# AI Execution Audit Platform (relaunching as Elvanis)

Full spec (BRD, architecture, DB schema, roadmap): [docs/AI-Execution-Audit-Platform-BRD-Architecture-Roadmap.md](docs/AI-Execution-Audit-Platform-BRD-Architecture-Roadmap.md)
Read that file in full before starting any non-trivial task — this file is a summary/index, not a replacement for it.

## What this is
A goal-driven AI execution audit platform for founder-led B2B SaaS / tech-enabled SMEs (20–200 employees). Client picks a business goal, submits evidence (native tool exports or fill-in templates — no live integrations in V1), and the system runs five parallel AI-drafted lenses, which a human reviewer must approve before a client ever sees them. Output is a financially-quantified top-3-priorities + 30/60/90 roadmap, with an AI Opportunity section gated by a readiness check.

**Relaunch decision:** this ships under the existing Elvanis brand/domain (`app.elvanis.com`), not a separate company — but on genuinely fresh infrastructure (new GitHub repo, new Supabase project, new Vercel project). The old Elvanis app/DB is untouched, kept as reference/rollback, never shared or reused. Deploy on Vercel's free URL during build/pilot; DNS cutover to `app.elvanis.com` is the last step, done only after `RESEND_FROM_EMAIL` is verified working on the new project.

Market sequencing: UK → Netherlands/Europe → Gulf (gated on UK/NL proof).

## Non-negotiable architecture principles
- **No hardcoded provider calls.** Every LLM call goes through the internal `ai-client` abstraction (see `lib/ai-client/`) so switching model/provider is a config change, not a multi-file rewrite. Elvanis was already forced through one painful Groq migration — don't repeat that.
- **Mandatory human review gate.** A `reports` row can never reach `sent` status without passing `pending_review` → reviewer `approved`. This is enforced at the DB/workflow level, not just a UI convention. Every finding is `draft` → `reviewer_edited` (optional) → `approved` before it can appear client-facing.
- **Isolated infrastructure.** New Supabase project, new env vars, new domain path. Never touch the old Elvanis DB/repo/tables.
- **Evidence-in, structured-out, per lens.** Each of the five lenses is an independent AI call with its own strict output schema — one lens failing must not block the others.
- **Business profile is a living record.** Every lens prompt reads the *current* profile/goal/evidence state at generation time, never a cached copy from signup. Changes are tracked in `company_profile_history`.
- **Source-agnostic evidence intake.** No OAuth/live integrations in V1. Per-lens native export upload (CSV/PDF) or fill-in template, reconciled via a merge-and-complete step. A known-source template library (Xero, QuickBooks, HubSpot, Salesforce, Jira, Intercom, Zendesk) auto-maps recognized files; unrecognized files fall back to generic parsing.

## Stack
- Next.js (App Router), TypeScript
- Supabase — **new isolated project** (DB/Auth/Storage) — credentials supplied by user, never reuse old Elvanis project
- Groq via `ai-client` abstraction (model/provider name lives in config/env)
- Resend for email (same pattern as old Elvanis)
- CSV parsing + PDF/text extraction + OCR fallback for evidence files

## Five lenses (equal depth, always)
Financial · Commercial/Market · Execution/Operating · Product/Customer · AI & Governance (questionnaire mode or document-review mode)

## Goal menu (locked 2026-07-31)
Cash Flow/Margin Efficiency · Growth/Revenue Efficiency · Churn/Retention · Execution Speed · Product Delivery. Goal is a weighting layer every lens reads, not a lens itself (spec §2.3 step 1) — no separate AI/Tender Readiness goal, that's the Tender Readiness module's own entry point. See `src/lib/lenses/goals.ts`.

## Service tiers, pricing, and onboarding structure (confirmed 2026-08-02)
Two Core Audit tiers, same technical pipeline (five-lens engine, Conflict Detection, mandatory review — no new pipeline for Concierge), different human attention. Full detail in spec §1.5/§1.9/§1.9a — summary:
- **Onboarding is two distinct things, not one**: the in-app **Guide** (self-serve help text, always on, every tier, no call, no gate before uploading) vs. the **Discovery Session** (optional live call — offered but never required on Standard; included by default on Concierge).
- **Delivery Session** (live call, post-report) is premium, not in the free/Standard tier (which is written report + dashboard only) — bundled by default with the paid Execution Sprint, or sellable standalone.
- **Concierge/White-Glove tier** (new): Discovery + Delivery Session both included by default, deeper reviewer attention to ambiguous findings, F2F Workshop available as a further upgrade. `users.plan_tier` (already in the schema, currently unused) is the natural home for this — not yet wired into any UI, including no tier badge in the Reviewer Workspace yet, so "deeper attention" is currently policy, not an enforced/visible signal.
- **F2F Workshop** is a premium upgrade *of* the Delivery Session specifically (in-person, multi-stakeholder) — never offered before evidence submission, since it needs real findings to discuss. No booking/scheduling UI exists yet to enforce this.
- **New, additive intake field, built 2026-08-02** (from gap-assessment-methodology research, not folded silently into existing goal capture): a per-goal, optional "in your own words, what would good look like here?" prompt — **always client-authored, no consultant-recorded version, one capture mechanism only**. Shown alongside each lens's benchmark comparison in the final report; when left blank, the report shows a short neutral note rather than silently omitting it (same "absence is itself a finding" pattern as missing financial visibility / missing AI governance docs) — documented in spec §1.9a, not yet implemented since the report generator itself doesn't exist yet. Schema: `goals.desired_future_state_primary` / `desired_future_state_secondary` (`supabase/migrations/20260802130000_goals_desired_future_state.sql`, pushed live). Validation is deliberately basic — length + a degenerate-repeated-character check ([src/lib/goals/validation.ts](src/lib/goals/validation.ts)), never an AI judgment of answer quality. `GoalContext` type updated to carry both fields, deliberately **not** wired into `formatGoalContextForPrompt` (lens prompts) — capture + display only, per explicit scope. **A real, working page exists and is verified live** ([src/app/(app)/business-profile/page.tsx](<src/app/(app)/business-profile/page.tsx>)) — addressed by `?goalId=` for now (same interim pre-auth pattern the Reviewer Workspace used before its own auth existed), since there's no client-auth system yet to resolve "the current client's company" from a session. The server action ([src/lib/goals/desired-future-state.ts](src/lib/goals/desired-future-state.ts)) uses the service-role client for the same reason — flagged in-code as a known, time-boxed gap to fix once client auth exists, not the intended long-term design. Confirmed live: saved a real answer against the seeded goal row, reloaded the page and confirmed it read back correctly, verified all 5 validation edge cases (blank/valid/too-long/spam/short-but-fine) directly.

## Modules built in V1 (research-driven, not client-trigger-gated)
Built on the shared core engine (evidence intake, reviewer workspace, report generator) once it's stable — that engine is done and proven (see the closed-out audit test below), so this is now active work. **Build order confirmed 2026-08-02: AI Reliability Audit → Tender Readiness → Data Protection Compliance** (reversed from the earlier Tender-Readiness-first ordering) — AI Reliability has the strongest validated commercial urgency (real documented cases — Air Canada, Cursor, 600+ sanctioned lawyers — versus Tender Readiness, whose urgency weakened once the EU AI Act's high-risk compliance deadline moved to December 2027). Data Protection stays last — least developed of the three, and the most commoditized domain.
- **AI Reliability Audit** — adversarial testing against documented real-world AI failure patterns. Confirmed design, spec §1.7a (**note:** this detail — conversational/agent-branching intake, evidence-based testing, the missing-trace-logs finding rule — existed only informally before 2026-08-02; searched the spec doc, this file, and the code stub and found none of it persisted, flagged that honestly rather than building on an unverifiable claim, and got it confirmed and written down for the first time here):
  - **Evidence-based, no live execution** — same pattern as every module/lens in V1, consistent with §1.7's no-live-integrations rule. Supersedes the older task-breakdown wording ("adversarial test execution against a client's live AI feature").
  - **Intake branches on system type** — a clarifying question first: conversational (chatbot) interface, or agent/automation running with no direct user-facing chat?
  - **Conversational path:** client runs a guided self-test script (specific adversarial prompts we provide, drawn from the documented failure patterns + 4 categories: invented policy, data leakage, bias, prompt injection), pastes back the real responses. Pure evidence submission, no API access.
  - **Agent/automation path:** intake asks for (a) trace logs/execution/incident history, (b) what credentials it operates under and whether its actions are attributable to it specifically, (c) whether a human escalation step exists for consequential actions.
  - **Deterministic guaranteed finding** (same "missing evidence is itself the finding" principle as Financial/AI & Governance): zero trace logs/execution history on the agent/automation path is itself a governance finding, injected in code, never left to LLM discretion.
  - **Core build done and verified live (2026-08-02)**: [src/lib/modules/ai-reliability-audit/](src/lib/modules/ai-reliability-audit/) — `types.ts` (findings reuse the same 4-field diagnosis/rootCause/recommendedAction/severity structure as `LensFinding`, no `goalRelevance`/`financialImpact` since this isn't a goal-weighted lens), `self-test-prompts.ts` (curated, deterministic — not LLM-generated — 7 prompts across the 4 categories, sourced from the Air Canada/Cursor patterns), `schemas.ts` + `index.ts` (conversational mode + agent/automation mode, mirroring AI & Governance's two-mode structure). Confirmed live: a genuine invented-policy failure was correctly flagged while two correctly-handled adversarial tests (data leakage, prompt injection) produced zero spurious findings; the agent/automation path's deterministic no-trace-logs finding fired correctly alongside real LLM-analyzed governance findings from the other evidence; the same path with trace logs present produced sensible, non-alarmist findings for a mostly-healthy setup.
  - **Real bug found and fixed in this same pass**: the agent/automation prompt explicitly told the model not to re-raise the "missing trace logs" finding (already guaranteed in code) — it did anyway, as a separately-worded duplicate ("Missing Trace Logs"). Same "prompt-only instruction isn't reliable" class of problem this codebase has hit repeatedly, fixed the same way each time: the trace-logs line is now omitted from the prompt entirely when absent (instead of describing it and asking the model not to comment), plus a deterministic `dropDuplicateTraceLogFindings()` backstop filters any LLM finding whose title/diagnosis/evidenceCited mentions trace logs when none were submitted. Re-tested the exact failing case afterward — clean, exactly one trace-log-related finding (the guaranteed one).
  - **Persistence, review integration, and a real entry page — all done (2026-08-02)**, using the new generic module-review architecture (see "Generic module review architecture" below) — no longer in-memory-only. A real submission was carried end-to-end: intake → `module_requests`/`module_findings` → mandatory review (accept the genuine failure, reject a spurious one, blocked-then-approved) → `approved`, confirmed live against real data.
  - **A second real bug found and fixed during this same live run**: even with rule 2 already telling the model not to manufacture findings for correctly-handled tests, a live submission (2 genuine `invented_policy` failures + 1 correctly-refused `data_leakage` test) produced a spurious third finding — "No Data Leakage Finding," describing the AI's *correct* refusal as if it were noteworthy. Same "prompt-only instruction isn't reliable" class of problem as the trace-logs duplication, but this one has no clean structural signal to filter deterministically (unlike hasTraceLogs' true/false). Fixed by restructuring the prompt to force an explicit silent PASS/FAIL classification per transcript before writing any findings, with a concrete negative example matching the exact observed failure ("do not write a finding titled anything like 'No Data Leakage Found'... silence is the correct output for a PASS") — re-tested the exact failing combination twice afterward, clean both times. The reviewer correctly rejected the original spurious finding as part of the same live run, which is exactly what the mandatory gate exists for — not treated as a system failure, treated as proof the gate works.
- **Tender Readiness** — AI-specific risk-classification content across all three researched jurisdictions, not EU AI Act only (confirmed design 2026-08-02, spec §1.8b, correcting the task-breakdown checklist's EU-only framing): EU AI Act (4-tier risk classification), UAE DIFC Regulation 10 (registration-triggered, DIFC free zone only — federal PDPL/ADGM DPR 2021 stay Data Protection Compliance's job, same AI-specific-vs-AI-agnostic domain split already used for §1.8a), Saudi AI governance (SDAIA's 7 principles + draft Responsible AI Policy, distinct from Saudi PDPL), and the non-binding UAE AI Charter as reference-only content. Applicability is deterministic — computed in code from `registration_country`/`uae_free_zone`/`customer_market_countries`, never AI-judged, same reasoning as the numeric-benchmark-comparison fix. Multiple sections can apply simultaneously. Also confirmed: a periodic regulatory-content-review flag (distinct from re-audit reminders — this is about the *regulatory content* going stale, not client data), reusing the `app_settings` cadence pattern.
  - **Deterministic jurisdiction logic built and unit-tested (2026-08-02)**: [src/lib/modules/tender-readiness/jurisdiction.ts](src/lib/modules/tender-readiness/jurisdiction.ts) — `computeJurisdictionApplicability()` reads `registrationCountry`/`uaeFreeZone`/`customerMarketCountries` and returns all four boolean flags independently (any combination can be true at once). ADGM has no AI-specific regime of its own (only DIFC Reg 10 is UAE's AI-specific rule) — an ADGM-registered company only gets the non-binding `uaeAiCharterReference` flag, never a fabricated "ADGM AI rule." Verified against a real, committed test suite — [src/lib/modules/tender-readiness/jurisdiction.test-cases.ts](src/lib/modules/tender-readiness/jurisdiction.test-cases.ts), runnable via `npx tsx --env-file=.env.local src/lib/modules/tender-readiness/jurisdiction.test-cases.ts` — covering exactly the founder-specified cases ("UK-registered with Saudi customers → PDPL/SDAIA but not DIFC," "DIFC-registered with no Gulf customers → DIFC Reg 10 but not EU AI Act") plus case-insensitivity, null registration, all-four-simultaneously, and ADGM-only. 10/10 passing.
  - **Module build and generic-architecture integration done (2026-08-02)**: [src/lib/modules/tender-readiness/](src/lib/modules/tender-readiness/) — `types.ts`/`schemas.ts` (same 4-field diagnosis/rootCause/recommendedAction/severity structure as every other module/lens), `index.ts` (`runTenderReadinessAudit()` computes applicability in code first, builds a prompt that lists only the applicable sections and forbids the model from discussing others, and injects `buildNoDocumentationFinding()` deterministically whenever no documentation was submitted for at least one substantive section — same "missing evidence is itself the finding" pattern used everywhere else), `persist.ts` (writes into the generic `module_requests`/`module_findings` tables, `module_type: "tender_readiness"`), standalone entry page ([src/app/(modules)/tender-readiness/page.tsx](<src/app/(modules)/tender-readiness/page.tsx>), `?companyId=`-addressed, same interim pattern as AI Reliability Audit) that computes and displays applicability to the client read-only before the intake form, rather than re-asking them to self-report it.
  - **Real bug found and fixed during live verification (2026-08-02), same "prompt-only instruction isn't reliable" class hit twice already in AI Reliability Audit**: a live submission with no documentation produced the correct deterministic `buildNoDocumentationFinding()` PLUS a second, separately-worded LLM finding — "Missing Documentation for EU AI Act Compliance" — re-raising the exact same gap. `buildPrompt()` had no rule telling the model that topic was already covered, unlike AI Reliability's agent-automation prompt which did. Fixed the same two-layer way as the trace-logs bug: (a) `buildPrompt()` now includes an explicit rule, only injected when `!hasDocs`, telling the model the missing-documentation gap is already guaranteed elsewhere and it must not write its own finding about missing/absent documentation; (b) a deterministic `dropDuplicateMissingDocumentationFindings()` backstop filters any LLM finding (other than the guaranteed one itself) whose title/diagnosis matches missing-documentation phrasing, when `!hasDocs`. Re-tested the exact failing evidence (UK-registered company, EU AI Act applicable, no documentation) directly against `runTenderReadinessAudit()` post-fix — exactly 2 findings, no duplicate — then re-verified through actual persistence (`runAndPersistTenderReadinessAudit()`), same clean result confirmed by direct DB read.
  - **Full reviewer workflow verified live end-to-end (2026-08-02)** against the clean post-fix request (`0e46e5dd-1596-468d-988e-2bcd1fb000ac`): `approveModuleRequest()` correctly blocked with `blockedReason` while both findings were still `draft`, `acceptModuleFinding()` (the guaranteed documentation finding) and `editModuleFinding()` (tightened wording on the EU AI Act classification finding) both persisted correctly, `approveModuleRequest()` then succeeded and stamped `reviewed_by`/`approved_at`, and the request correctly dropped out of the `pending_review` queue afterward — same mandatory-gate discipline already proven for the core audit and AI Reliability Audit.
  - **Periodic regulatory-content-review flag** ([src/lib/reviewer/regulatory-content-review.ts](src/lib/reviewer/regulatory-content-review.ts), `regulatory_content_reviews` table) — a simple per-jurisdiction `last_reviewed_at` date, tied to the existing `app_settings` cadence pattern (`regulatory_content_review_days`, default 180), flagging when regulatory reference content is overdue for a human re-check; this is a manual-process signal, nothing in the code can detect a law changing on its own. `checkRegulatoryContentReviewDue()` (cron-facing, idempotent per overdue cycle via `notified_at`) was built and verified live 2026-08-02, but **only ever covered Tender Readiness's three sections (eu_ai_act/uae_difc_reg10/saudi_ai_governance) — Data Protection Compliance's regulations (uk_gdpr/eu_gdpr, and now saudi_pdpl) were never added, a real gap confirmed by `grep` showing zero UI references anywhere and only 3 seeded rows, not 6.** Closed 2026-08-03: [supabase/migrations/20260803090000_regulatory_content_review_gdpr_pdpl.sql](supabase/migrations/20260803090000_regulatory_content_review_gdpr_pdpl.sql) adds the missing 3 rows (`uk_gdpr`/`eu_gdpr` seeded 2026-07-31, the actual §1.8a/§1.8c research date; `saudi_pdpl` seeded 2026-08-03, when it was actually researched) — the underlying mechanism needed zero code changes to support them, confirming it was built generically the first time. **Also newly built (2026-08-03): the display was never surfaced visibly anywhere** — only the backend cron/notification path existed. Added `listRegulatoryContentReviewStatus()` (read-only, no side effects, distinct from the notification-firing `checkRegulatoryContentReviewDue()`) and a "Regulatory content status" panel on `/queue` ([src/app/(reviewer)/queue/page.tsx](<src/app/(reviewer)/queue/page.tsx>)) listing all 6 jurisdictions with last-reviewed date, days-since, and an overdue flag, plus a "Mark reviewed" button per row (a new `markRegulatoryContentReviewedAction` in [src/app/(reviewer)/queue/actions.ts](<src/app/(reviewer)/queue/actions.ts>), same independent session+role re-check pattern as every other reviewer Server Action) — closing the loop on `markRegulatoryContentReviewed()`, which existed with zero callers until now. Reviewer-facing, not client-facing, per the mechanism's own original design intent ("this is a content-maintenance task, not something a client sees"). Verified via direct function calls (no live browser session was active in this pass — same acceptable-risk precedent as the "authenticated but wrong role" denial branch, given the JSX is a simple map matching the already-proven pattern used for the queue's own report/module list): `listRegulatoryContentReviewStatus()` correctly returned all 6 rows with accurate days-since/overdue math, and `markRegulatoryContentReviewed("saudi_pdpl", ...)` correctly reset its `last_reviewed_at` to now and `daysSinceReview` to 0.
- **Data Protection Compliance** — standalone, same commercial pattern as Tender Readiness (post-audit by default, standalone if externally triggered). GDPR-first build order (spec §1.8a/§1.8d, confirmed design 2026-08-02), **extended 2026-08-03 with a real Saudi PDPL branch** — broader and AI-agnostic (applies to any company handling personal data, AI or not), genuinely distinct from Tender Readiness's AI-specific governance/risk-classification content. **Correction, 2026-08-03: Saudi PDPL is not Gulf-entry-gated** — it's already actively enforced today, unlike the UAE's data-protection regime (federal PDPL, ADGM DPR 2021), which genuinely is gated on real UAE client exposure and remains deferred. The earlier framing treated both as equally deferred, which was wrong. Five core checklist categories, unchanged from the original §1.8a/§5 scope, apply to every regulation (GDPR variants and PDPL alike): consent-flow review, data-subject-rights readiness (access/correct/delete/port), retention policy review, breach-response readiness, cross-border transfer check.
  - **Deterministic jurisdiction logic built and unit-tested, extended with PDPL 2026-08-03**: [src/lib/modules/data-protection-compliance/jurisdiction.ts](src/lib/modules/data-protection-compliance/jurisdiction.ts) — `computeJurisdictionApplicability()` (renamed from `computeGdprApplicability()` once PDPL made the old name inaccurate) returns three regime flags (`ukGdpr`, `euGdpr`, `saudiPdpl`), each with the same establishment-OR-extraterritorial-reach structure. **Real correction made to the original §1.8a/§1.8c research while first implementing this (2026-08-02)**: both docs framed GDPR as triggered only by `customer_market_countries` — but GDPR Article 3 actually has two independent triggers, and the customer-market-only framing would have silently missed a UK-registered company selling only to non-UK/EU customers, still bound by UK GDPR via establishment (Article 3(1)) alone. Saudi PDPL has the identical two-trigger structure (established/residing in Saudi Arabia, or extraterritorial reach to Saudi residents), so the same fix applies directly — confirmed and implemented, not assumed. **A second correction made while adding PDPL (2026-08-03)**: the original §1.8 forward-looking note claimed PDPL's 72-hour breach-notification window is "stricter than a GDPR-only posture" — false; GDPR/UK GDPR Article 33 also requires 72-hour regulator notification where feasible. Both spec doc passages fixed inline, not silently changed. Verified against a real, committed test suite — [src/lib/modules/data-protection-compliance/jurisdiction.test-cases.ts](src/lib/modules/data-protection-compliance/jurisdiction.test-cases.ts), runnable via `npx tsx --env-file=.env.local src/lib/modules/data-protection-compliance/jurisdiction.test-cases.ts` — now 12/12 passing, including the PDPL-specific cases: a UK-registered company with Saudi customers (PDPL via extraterritorial reach alongside UK GDPR via establishment, not EU GDPR — the data-protection-domain parallel to Tender Readiness's own original "UK-registered with Saudi customers" test case), a Saudi-registered company with no other markets (PDPL via establishment alone), a non-Saudi-registered company with only Saudi customers (PDPL via extraterritorial reach alone), and all three regimes applying simultaneously.
  - **Divergence-note logic generalized from a single boolean to a composable function** (`buildDivergenceNote()`) to handle up to three simultaneously-applicable regulations instead of one UK/EU pairwise check. When Saudi PDPL applies alongside either GDPR variant, the prompt now explicitly flags SDAIA as a distinct regulator and PDPL's cross-border transfer mechanism (SDAIA-recognized adequacy/approved contractual safeguards) as genuinely separate machinery from GDPR's SCC/adequacy-decision framework — and explicitly instructs the model not to describe PDPL's breach-notification timeframe as "stricter" than GDPR's, since both use 72 hours. **Verified live**: a direct call to `runDataProtectionComplianceAudit()` with a UK-registered company + Saudi customers (`ukGdpr: true, euGdpr: false, saudiPdpl: true`) and evidence describing an incident-response doc that didn't specify which regulator to notify per customer base produced a real LLM finding — "Insufficient Breach Response Regulator Specification" — that correctly diagnosed exactly that gap and recommended clarifying by customer location, with no duplicate or hallucinated findings across the 5 total findings returned (3 deterministic missing-evidence + 2 genuine).
  - **Shared regions module extracted during this build** ([src/lib/modules/shared/regions.ts](src/lib/modules/shared/regions.ts)): the EU-member-states list is identical data both Tender Readiness and this module need — rather than let two copies exist and risk drift, `EU_MEMBER_STATES`/`UK_NAMES`/`SAUDI_ARABIA_NAMES`/`UAE_NAMES`/`normalize()` were extracted to one shared file. Tender Readiness's `jurisdiction.ts` was refactored to import from it instead of its own local copy — re-ran its full 10/10 test suite immediately after to confirm zero regression.
  - **Module build and generic-architecture integration done (2026-08-02)**: [src/lib/modules/data-protection-compliance/](src/lib/modules/data-protection-compliance/) — `types.ts`/`schemas.ts` (same 4-field diagnosis/rootCause/recommendedAction/severity structure as every other module/lens, findings tagged by `category` — one of the five checklist areas — and `applicableRegulations`), `index.ts` (`runDataProtectionComplianceAudit()` computes applicability in code first, then per-category: a blank category gets a deterministic guaranteed missing-evidence finding, a category with evidence goes to the LLM). When both UK GDPR and EU GDPR apply simultaneously, the prompt explicitly flags where they diverge procedurally despite being near-identical in substance — breach notification (ICO vs. the relevant national supervisory authority, potentially two regulators to notify) and cross-border transfer adequacy mechanisms (UK's own adequacy regulations vs. the EU Commission's, diverged post-Brexit) — rather than treating the two regimes as interchangeable. `persist.ts` writes into the generic `module_requests`/`module_findings` tables, `module_type: "data_protection"`. Standalone entry page ([src/app/(modules)/data-protection-compliance/page.tsx](<src/app/(modules)/data-protection-compliance/page.tsx>), `?companyId=`-addressed, same interim pattern as the other two modules) computes and displays applicable regimes to the client read-only before a 5-field intake form (one per checklist category — a blank field is itself meaningful evidence, not an incomplete submission).
  - **Duplicate-finding safeguard built in proactively, not discovered live** — this exact failure mode (the LLM re-raising a topic already covered by a deterministic guaranteed finding) had already recurred three times across AI Reliability Audit (twice) and Tender Readiness (once), each fixed only after being caught live. For this module, both halves of the fix were built from the start rather than waited on: (a) `buildPrompt()` explicitly lists which categories had no evidence and instructs the model not to write findings about them, and (b) `dropDuplicateMissingEvidenceFindings()` deterministically drops any LLM-produced finding whose `category` field matches a blank category — a cleaner backstop than Tender Readiness's regex-based one, since `category` is a structured field here rather than free text to pattern-match.
  - **Verified live end-to-end, 2026-08-02, real submission against Nimbus Ledger Ltd (UK-registered, UK+Netherlands customer markets — both `ukGdpr`/`euGdpr` correctly true)**: 3 of 5 categories filled with real evidence (consent flow, data-subject rights, retention policy), 2 left deliberately blank (breach response, cross-border transfer) to exercise the missing-evidence path. Result was clean on the first submission, no bug-fix cycle needed this time: exactly 5 findings — 2 deterministic guaranteed missing-evidence findings (one each for the blank categories) and 3 genuine LLM findings (one per category with evidence), zero duplicates, every finding correctly tagged `applicableRegulations: ["uk_gdpr", "eu_gdpr"]` (request `d218a631-c30f-4b9c-8c3b-5e7e8185cab1`). Full reviewer workflow then run against this real request via direct calls to `module-workspace.ts`: `approveModuleRequest()` correctly blocked with `blockedReason` while all 5 findings were `draft`, then all three dispositions were exercised (`acceptModuleFinding` ×3, `editModuleFinding` ×1 on the consent-flow finding, `rejectModuleFinding` ×1 on the data-subject-rights finding with reviewer reasoning), `approveModuleRequest()` then succeeded and stamped `reviewed_by`/`approved_at`, and the request correctly dropped out of the `pending_review` queue afterward — same mandatory-gate discipline already proven for the core audit and the other two modules.

Each has its own intake, findings schema, report template, and standalone entry page.

## Generic module review architecture (confirmed 2026-08-02)
AI Reliability Audit, Tender Readiness, and Data Protection Compliance all hit the same question: how do their findings get reviewed? Solved generically once here, not as a one-off for AI Reliability with the other two repeating the question right behind it — same principle already used for AI & Governance not being forced into the shared `LensModule` interface: **reuse the review mechanism, don't force the findings shape.**

**Schema** (`supabase/migrations/20260802140000_generic_module_review.sql`) — replaces the three separate `*_requests` tables (`tender_readiness_requests`, `ai_reliability_requests`, `data_protection_requests`), all three still completely empty and unreferenced by any code (a v0 scaffold predating the lens architecture's findings-structure lessons), so dropping and replacing needed no backfill:
- **`module_requests`** — plays the same role `reports` plays for the core audit: the review-gate container, reusing the exact same `report_status` enum (draft/pending_review/approved/sent) and the exact same "sent requires a reviewer" DB check constraint. `intake_data` (jsonb) holds whatever raw intake shape that module needs — genuinely module-specific, doesn't generalize (AI Reliability's is `{systemType, conversationalTranscripts, agentAutomationEvidence}`; Tender Readiness/Data Protection will have their own shapes).
- **`module_findings`** — plays the same role `lens_findings` plays, reusing the exact same `reviewer_status` enum (draft/edited/approved/rejected) and the same immutable-`ai_draft`-vs-`reviewer_edited_content` pattern. Deliberately does **not** carry `goalRelevance`/`financialImpact`/`origin`/dispute fields — those are core-audit-specific, not generic to every module. `ai_draft` still follows the same `diagnosis`/`rootCause`/`recommendedAction`/`severity` structure as `LensFinding` (that separation is the core value proposition, not something to regress on), just without the fields that don't apply.

**Review mechanism** ([src/lib/reviewer/module-workspace.ts](src/lib/reviewer/module-workspace.ts)) — `acceptModuleFinding`/`editModuleFinding`/`rejectModuleFinding`/`approveModuleRequest`/`deliverModuleRequest`, mirroring `workspace.ts` function-for-function (same mandatory-gate logic: `approveModuleRequest` is blocked, not just discouraged, until every finding has a disposition). Deliberately omits `resolveConflict`/`resolveDispute` — those are cross-lens concepts (findings from different lenses contradicting each other, a client disputing an `ai_independent` Commercial finding) that don't apply within one standalone module's own findings.

**One unified reviewer queue, not three separate interfaces** — `/queue` now lists Core Audit reports and every module's pending requests together, sorted into one list, each linking to the right workspace (`/review/[reportId]` for reports, the new `/review-module/[requestId]` for modules). The module workspace UI mirrors the core workspace's Accept/Edit/Reject/mandatory-decision-banner pattern but flat (no lens grouping, since a module has one finding set, not five).

**Confirmed live end-to-end (2026-08-02)** against a real AI Reliability Audit submission: intake → `module_requests`/`module_findings` persisted → Approve correctly blocked with 2 undecided findings → Accept (genuine failure) + Reject (a spurious finding, see below) → Approve succeeded, `reviewed_by`/`approved_at` stamped → request correctly dropped out of the pending queue query afterward. Verified via direct calls to the same functions the Server Actions call, not re-driven through a fresh browser reviewer sign-in — reviewer auth itself (magic-link/code/session/role-gate) was already thoroughly proven live earlier this session on the core workspace, and this round focused on proving the new generic mechanism, not re-proving auth. The queue page's own JSX rendering was not re-confirmed via a fresh browser click-through this round (the underlying query was verified directly, and the JSX is the same proven pattern already used for reports) — worth a quick visual check next time the queue is open in a live session, not a known gap, just not re-clicked-through today.

## Information architecture — four separate pages, not one
- **Account Settings** — the person (name/email/password/notifications/billing)
- **Business Profile** — the company being diagnosed (living record every lens reads from)
- **Reports & History** — chronological, frozen-snapshot archive of every generated report
- **Dashboard** — current live state (latest top-3, roadmap status, active sprint)

## Database schema
Full schema is in §3 of the spec doc. Core tables: `users`, `companies`, `company_profile_history`, `digital_presence_scans`, `goals`, `evidence_submissions`, `evidence_files`, `evidence_fields`, `lens_findings`, `financial_impact_estimates`, `ai_governance_detail`, `ai_opportunity_synthesis`, `readiness_scores`, `priority_ranking`, `finding_conflicts`, `reports`, `notifications`, `execution_sprints`, `sprint_tasks`, `scheduled_jobs`, `case_library`, `monthly_retainers` (V3), `export_source_signatures`, `app_settings`, `module_requests`, `module_findings` (generic across AI Reliability/Tender Readiness/Data Protection — see "Generic module review architecture" below).

`companies.country` was split into `registration_country` + `uae_free_zone` (mainland/difc/adgm) and `customer_market_countries` (text[]) — see "Multi-jurisdiction regulatory landscape" below for why.

## Multi-jurisdiction regulatory landscape
Full research and sourcing in spec §1.8c — read that before building any jurisdiction-aware logic in Tender Readiness, AI & Governance, or Data Protection Compliance. Tender Readiness's own confirmed applicability design (which sections, the deterministic-in-code rule, the domain boundary vs. Data Protection Compliance) is spec §1.8b. Summary: regulatory applicability is two independent signals, not one "country" field. EU AI Act / GDPR / Saudi PDPL are extraterritorial (triggered by where *customers* are); UAE's DIFC Regulation 10 / ADGM rules are triggered by where the *company is registered* — both can apply to the same company at once, hence the `companies` schema split above. There is no single "UAE AI Act" — it's a layered regime (federal PDPL, DIFC Reg 10, ADGM DPR 2021, non-binding UAE AI Charter, plus a new Federal Authority for AI and Data as of June 2026). Saudi Arabia has AI-specific governance (SDAIA) separate from PDPL, and is still actively evolving (a live National AI Risk Management Framework plus a still-draft Responsible AI Policy) — re-verify before Gulf entry rather than trusting this as final.

## SLA, submission flow & business rules (confirmed 2026-07-31)
Full detail in spec §1.5 (free tier), §2.1 (no-auto-approve principle), §2.3a (SLA/submission flow), §2.5 (one company per account), §3 (schema). Summary:
- **72h SLA, one number to the client** ("ready within 72 hours"), internally 24h edit window + 48h review period that only starts once the window closes. The reviewer notification must fire the instant the 24h window closes (a `scheduled_jobs` trigger) so the 48h clock is accurate. **Verification pass, 2026-08-03**: the 24h edit window is real and correctly computed (`EDIT_WINDOW_HOURS = 24` in `run-audit.ts`), and the reviewer-queue listing/notification timing both correctly gate on it — but the 48h review period exists only as narrative, never as an enforced deadline anywhere in code (no `review_due_at` field, no SLA-breach check; duration is measured after approval, not tracked against a target). **Real gap found and fixed same day**: `approveReport()` never checked `edit_window_closes_at` at all — only the Reviewer Queue's own listing filter hid early reports, so a reviewer navigating directly to `/review/[reportId]` by URL could approve during the client's still-open edit window. Fixed: `approveReport()` (`src/lib/reviewer/workspace.ts`) now loads and checks `edit_window_closes_at` as a real gate before anything else, returning `blockedReason` if the window hasn't closed. Verified live: a synthetic report with a future `edit_window_closes_at` was correctly blocked, then correctly approved once backdated. Also verified client-facing SLA copy is internally consistent (the demo prototype's submit-confirmation modal always pairs "24 hours to edit" with "72 hours total," matching spec §2.3a's own modal text exactly) — the spec doc itself had a self-contradiction (line 241 claimed the 24h breakdown is "never exposed" while line 243, two lines later, specified modal text that discloses it) — corrected in the spec doc, scoping the "single number" framing to headline/marketing copy only, since the code (and spec's own modal text) was right to disclose it at the actual submit decision point. **Superseded 2026-08-06**: `EDIT_WINDOW_HOURS` is no longer a hardcoded constant, and — a real, separate gap found in the same pass — the confirmation modal described above only ever existed in the demo prototype; the real live evidence-intake flow had no modal at all until this date. See "EDIT_WINDOW_HOURS migrated to DB + the real confirmation modal finally built" below for the full closure.
- **"Submit for Review" is an explicit action with a confirmation modal**, separate from uploading (which has no clock). Modal text and exact behavior in spec §2.3a.
- **New evidence after a report is delivered starts a new re-audit cycle**, never a silent edit — reuses the existing re-audit cron; the original report stays frozen in Reports & History.
- **Free tier = first completed audit per company only.** Re-audits are always paid.
- **One company per account (V1)** — DB-enforced via a unique constraint on `companies.user_id`. Does NOT mean one country: `registration_country`/`uae_free_zone` and `customer_market_countries` are independent fields that can differ for the same company; any regulatory check must read them independently, never assume they match. (Note: these fields are named `registration_country` + `uae_free_zone` in the actual schema, not `registration_jurisdiction` — same concept, flagging the naming since a recent conversation referenced the latter.)
- **No auto-approve mechanism yet, deliberately** — the right granularity (per-lens? per-finding-type? per-confidence-threshold?) isn't known yet, and guessing risks rebuilding later. Mandatory review is unchanged for every finding, no exceptions. Instead, `lens_findings` now captures pure data for a future decision: `origin` (`client_reported`/`ai_independent`), `client_confidence_marking` (`accurate`/`not_confident`), `is_disputed`, `dispute_resolution_notes` — alongside the existing `confidence_level`/`reviewer_status`. Zero behavior change; this is data capture only.

## Current phase
**Phase 2 has started (confirmed 2026-08-01).** All five core lenses were finished in Phase 0/1 (2026-07-31), and the founder explicitly moved into Phase 2 build immediately after: Conflict Detection, AI Opportunity Synthesis, the full Reviewer Workspace (actions + UI), and the Reviewer Queue are now built — see "Phase 2: Conflict Detection, AI Opportunity Synthesis, Reviewer Workspace" below. This is a real phase transition, not scope creep — "no UI yet by design" was a Phase 0/1 boundary, not a permanent one.

**Pre-launch hardening — 2 of 3 done (confirmed 2026-08-02).** With Phase 2 proven end-to-end (all 5 lenses, real conflicts, real synthesis, a full reviewer pass — see the closed-out audit test below), the founder confirmed the core five-lens engine is ready for real pilots and set the order for what's left before going live: (1) reviewer authentication — a precondition, not a feature, since real pilot data is about to touch a system with zero access control; (2) a production cron scheduler, which also properly fixes the AI Opportunity Synthesis persistence gap found in the last audit run; (3) then the three modules, in this order: AI Reliability Audit → Tender Readiness → Data Protection Compliance (reordered 2026-08-02 from the earlier Tender-Readiness-first plan — see "Modules built in V1" above for reasoning), built in parallel with/shortly after (1) and (2) rather than delaying real pilot testing of what already works. Both (1) and (2) are done — see "Reviewer authentication" and "Production cron scheduler" below. **All three modules — AI Reliability Audit, Tender Readiness, and Data Protection Compliance — are now built and verified live (confirmed 2026-08-02). Pre-launch hardening is complete.**

**Infrastructure — complete:**
- **Next.js 16** (App Router, TypeScript, Tailwind, `src/` dir) — builds and typechecks clean
- **Supabase**: full DB schema live on the new project (`pcnbekntpeqnacaaatkl` / DB name `elvanis-v2`), pushed via `supabase db push`. RLS scopes every company-owned table to `companies.user_id = auth.uid()`; a DB-level check constraint (`reports_sent_requires_reviewer`) enforces the mandatory review gate. `.env.local` has real `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL` / `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` / `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` (gitignored, not committed)
- **Groq**: `GROQ_API_KEY` set and verified live against `api.groq.com/openai/v1/models`; only reachable through `src/lib/ai-client`
- **GitHub**: repo pushed to [github.com/sallyabas/elvanis-v2](https://github.com/sallyabas/elvanis-v2), `main` branch, tracked
- **Email — both addresses confirmed working end-to-end** (real test sends received and checked, not just API 200s):
  - Supabase auth emails: custom SMTP configured via Resend's relay (`smtp.resend.com:465`, sender `noreply@app.elvanis.com`, sender name "Elvanis") — a live magic-link OTP send was received with the correct sender
  - Transactional email: `RESEND_FROM_EMAIL=info@app.elvanis.com`, domain verified in Resend (sending enabled) — a live test send was received with the correct sender
- Route skeleton: `src/app/(app)/{dashboard,business-profile,reports,account-settings}` and `src/app/(modules)/{tender-readiness,ai-reliability-audit,data-protection-compliance}` — placeholder pages only
- Lib skeleton: `src/lib/{lenses,evidence,reviewer,reports,modules}` — typed stubs that throw "not yet implemented," marking exactly what Phase 2 needs to fill in

**Lens prompt design — all 5 of 5 done (2026-07-31):**
- **Financial** ([src/lib/lenses/financial.ts](src/lib/lenses/financial.ts)) — done, tested live against Groq incl. sparse/contradictory evidence. Benchmarks in `financial-benchmarks.ts` are founder-set starting points (gross margin, runway, customer concentration), explicitly provisional pending real pilot data.
- **Execution/Operating** ([src/lib/lenses/execution.ts](src/lib/lenses/execution.ts)) — done, tested. Benchmarks in `execution-benchmarks.ts` are externally published (DORA 2025, LinearB 2026, Agile IG/West Monroe decision-latency research, aggregated meeting-load surveys) since the founder has no first-hand thresholds for this domain yet — every figure is sourced, marked provisional.
- **AI & Governance** ([src/lib/lenses/ai-governance.ts](src/lib/lenses/ai-governance.ts)) — done, tested (questionnaire mode with/without live AI, document-review mode). Confirmed scope: this lens does NOT produce the AI Opportunity output (that's a separate cross-lens synthesis step, built later, per spec §2.3 step 6) and does NOT do formal EU AI Act conformity/risk classification (that's the standalone Tender Readiness module's job) — it only assesses AI governance maturity. Deliberately does not implement the shared `LensModule` interface — its input/output shape genuinely differs (mode branch, per-dimension maturity scores), and forcing it into the same interface as Financial/Execution would have been a worse abstraction than being honest it's shaped differently. See "AI & Governance architecture" below.
- **Commercial/Market** ([src/lib/lenses/commercial.ts](src/lib/lenses/commercial.ts)) — hybrid design built and fully verified live end-to-end (confirmed 2026-07-31): client self-report first (named competitors, market change/pricing-pressure/lost-deals notes), then independent research (targeted searches on named competitors + a broader scan, via real Tavily calls — no mocked data). Findings are `origin`-tagged `client_reported`/`ai_independent`; the client marks confidence on `ai_independent` findings, and a `not_confident` marking drops it from client view while surfacing it to the reviewer as disputed (`is_disputed`/`dispute_resolution_notes`, set later by that interaction, not by the lens). Also does not implement the shared `LensModule` interface, same reasoning as AI & Governance. Self-report-only path tested clean (no fabrication, correct tagging, an earlier spurious "no independent research was run" finding was caught and fixed).
  - **Origin tagging is validated deterministically, not left to the LLM** — this is now closed out, same treatment as the numeric-comparison fix, not left as a "measurably improved" prompt tweak. Live testing found findings citing both self-report AND independent-research evidence under one origin tag, undermining the client-confidence-marking/dispute mechanism the tag exists for; a prompt-only rule (2a) measurably improved but did not eliminate it. Fixed properly: evidence is now cited using structured, parseable keys (`self_report.*` / `independent_research.N`) instead of freeform strings, and `classifyAndSplitFindings()` deterministically checks every finding's citations against its declared origin after the LLM responds — a finding spanning both categories gets split into two correctly-tagged findings (titled "— as reported by client" / "— per independent research"), and a finding citing only one category gets its origin corrected regardless of what the LLM declared. Unit-tested with 7 assertions covering the exact observed bug pattern, a fully-mistagged finding, and an unrecognized-citation passthrough case — all pass. Re-ran the live mixed-origin test case that originally exposed the bug: all 4 findings came back cleanly single-origin, and the split/correction logic is proven to catch it even when they don't.
  - **Independent research is real and confirmed live** ([src/lib/lenses/commercial-research.ts](src/lib/lenses/commercial-research.ts)): "the system runs its own research" is built on `src/lib/search-client` (Tavily, same pattern as `ai-client` for Groq — provider-swappable, nothing outside `search-client` imports the Tavily API directly). `runCompetitorResearch()` runs one bounded targeted search per client-named competitor plus one broader independent scan, returning results straight from the provider with real `sourceUrls` — nothing LLM-generated. `TAVILY_API_KEY` is set and verified live (direct API call + `search()` + `runCompetitorResearch()` all tested end-to-end, real results with real URLs). The broader-scan query is somewhat generic right now and sometimes surfaces generic marketing-blog content rather than genuine competitive intel — a search-query quality issue worth refining later, not a correctness bug (the lens correctly marked that content low-relevance). The lens itself takes already-gathered `IndependentResearchFinding[]` as input rather than calling this itself, so its synthesis logic didn't need to change once search was wired up.
- **Product/Customer** ([src/lib/lenses/product.ts](src/lib/lenses/product.ts)) — done, tested (rich evidence + sparse/vague evidence). Benchmarks in `product-benchmarks.ts` are externally published (Recurly 2025 Churn Report, Retently/SurveySparrow/Sybill 2026 NPS benchmarks, Userpilot 2024/2025 Product Metrics Report, 2025 SaaS activation-rate aggregates, 2025/2026 SaaS CSAT reports) since the founder has no first-hand thresholds for this domain either — all 21 deterministic tier-boundary cases unit-tested and passing. Explicit boundary rules: must not produce deep financial-metrics diagnosis (Financial's job) or process-speed diagnosis like response/resolution time (Execution's job) — this lens owns product usage/adoption, satisfaction, and churn as a *product-fit* signal, not the money or the process clock around it. **Two minor non-determinism observations from testing, not new bugs**: (1) the model sometimes omits the exact computed-comparison numbers from a finding's `rootCause` even though the tier classification itself (in the title) is always correct — same accepted category as Execution's occasional dropped findings; (2) once, a vague self-reported claim ("customers seem happy") was surfaced as its own low-confidence finding rather than folded into missing-data handling, similar in kind to (but milder than) the bug already fixed in Financial. Both are within the range of variability the mandatory review gate exists to catch — not re-tested further to conserve Groq quota.

**Search-client architecture** ([src/lib/search-client/](src/lib/search-client/)): mirrors `ai-client` exactly — `types.ts` (provider interface), `config.ts` (provider/model from env), `providers/tavily.ts` (only file allowed to call the Tavily API), `index.ts` (the `search()` entry point every caller must use). Currently the only caller is Commercial/Market's independent research step. `SEARCH_PROVIDER=tavily` and `TAVILY_API_KEY` set in `.env.local` and verified live (2026-07-31) — direct API call, `search()`, and `runCompetitorResearch()` all confirmed working with real results/URLs.

**Demo prototype** ([src/app/(demo)/demo/page.tsx](<src/app/(demo)/demo/page.tsx>)) — confirmed 2026-07-31, built to safely test the submit/edit-window/free-tier flow before any real client sees it (doubles as a grant/incubator demo). Mock data, NOT connected to Supabase — a deliberate, explicit exception to "no UI yet," not a precedent for building more UI early. Working end-to-end, tested live in-browser (not just typechecked): Submit for Review → confirmation modal (exact copy from spec §2.3a) → 24h edit window countdown → reviewer-notified log line firing exactly at the window boundary → 48h review (auto-approved for demo purposes) → delivered report in Reports & History → uploading new evidence after delivery starts a new re-audit cycle, correctly tagged "Paid re-audit" while the original stays untouched and tagged "Free audit." Timers are compressed and configurable (default: 1 real second = 1 simulated hour, so the real 24h/48h/72h numbers play out at exactly 1/3600 scale — a 72-second run-through *is* the same 72 hours, just faster). One real bug caught and fixed here: an early version called `setState` directly inside a 100ms-ticking effect (flagged by `react-hooks/set-state-in-effect`) — fixed by moving all state updates into the `setInterval` callback itself (the idiomatic "external system" pattern) using refs to avoid stale closures, rather than deriving transitions via a second effect reacting to state.

**Cross-lens patterns established (apply to all future lenses):**
- Shared schema lives in `src/lib/lenses/types.ts` (`LensFinding`, `GoalContext`, etc.) — don't reinvent per lens.
- `generateValidatedJson` (in `ai-client`) + a zod schema per lens is mandatory, not `generateJson`. Found live during Financial testing: Groq's `json_object` mode guarantees valid JSON but not conformance to our enums — it hallucinated `goalRelevance: "directly_affects"` (not a valid value) on two independent runs. Validation now fails loudly instead of letting bad data through.
- **Numeric benchmark comparisons are computed in code, never by the LLM** — this is now a closed-out fix, not an open risk. Testing found the model calling 96 hours "slower than industry average" against a 105.6-hour benchmark (96 < 105.6, actually faster) — a prompt-only "show your work" fix made the error auditable but did NOT reliably fix the model's own conclusion (still wrong in 2 of 3 runs). Fixed properly via `src/lib/lenses/metrics.ts`: lenses take a typed `metrics: MetricInput[]` array (metric key + raw value) alongside free-text `evidenceFields`; each lens's benchmarks file exports a `compare*Metric(key, value)` function that does the actual >, <, tier-lookup comparison deterministically, returning a `ComputedMetricComparison` with the comparison sentence already written correctly, injected into the prompt as a "COMPUTED BENCHMARK COMPARISONS — already calculated, do not recompute" block. The LLM's job is only to narrate implications, never judge direction/units itself. Re-tested the exact failing case (96h vs 105.6h) 4 times post-fix — correct every time. Applied to Financial (`compareFinancialMetric`) and Execution (`compareExecutionMetric`). One related bug caught in the same pass: when `evidenceFields` was empty, the prompt said "no evidence submitted" even when `metrics` had data — misled the model into an unwarranted "insufficient evidence" verdict. Fixed the wording to point at the computed-comparisons section instead.
- A lens must respect boundaries with sibling lenses (e.g. Financial must NOT diagnose *why* financial data is missing — that's Execution's operating-maturity-gap territory; Execution DOES own "no financial visibility"-type findings for exactly that reason). AI & Governance similarly must not duplicate Tender Readiness's deep AI Act work, and must not produce AI Opportunity output.
- **"Missing evidence is itself a finding, deterministically guaranteed when the condition is structural"** — pattern generalized from Financial to AI & Governance: if a specific known-risk combination exists (e.g. live AI in production + zero governance docs), don't just prompt the LLM to remember to flag it — inject the finding in code after generation, so it's never silently dropped. The LLM has been observed dropping findings non-deterministically across repeated runs (e.g. Execution occasionally omitted a PR-pickup-time finding even when the metric was provided) — do not rely on prompt instructions alone for anything that must always appear.

**AI & Governance architecture** ([src/lib/lenses/ai-governance-framework.ts](src/lib/lenses/ai-governance-framework.ts), [src/lib/lenses/ai-governance.ts](src/lib/lenses/ai-governance.ts)):
- 7-dimension maturity rubric (AI use inventory, risk classification awareness, human oversight, data governance for AI, vendor/model risk management, incident response & monitoring, governance ownership), each dimension scored 0-3, sourced from EU AI Act (4-tier risk classification, Art. 14 human oversight), NIST AI RMF (Govern/Map/Measure/Manage), ISO/IEC 42001 (management-system clauses), and OECD AI Principles (accountability) — externally researched per founder's direction, same treatment as Execution's benchmarks, explicitly provisional.
- Mode is decided by whether governance documents were submitted (`governanceDocsSubmitted`), per the original spec framing — NOT by whether the company has live AI in production. `hasLiveAiInProduction` is captured as an independent signal regardless of mode.
- **Questionnaire mode** (no docs): dimension scores are supplied by the client/reviewer and looked up deterministically in code (`scoreDimension`) — fully deterministic, same pattern as `metrics.ts`.
- **Document-review mode** (docs submitted): the LLM can't be handed a pre-computed score since the evidence is unstructured documents — but its role is narrowed to a classification task (pick the 0-3 integer whose rubric description best matches the evidence), not free-form scoring. The canonical level-description text is still always looked up in code from that integer, never generated by the model. Overall maturity aggregation (`computeOverallMaturity`) is the same deterministic function in both modes.
- `dimensionScores` (all 7, always) is returned separately from `findings` (LLM-curated, may not cover every weak dimension) — so a reviewer never loses visibility into a zero-scored dimension even when the LLM's prose findings didn't call it out.

## Phase 2: Conflict Detection, AI Opportunity Synthesis, Reviewer Workspace (confirmed 2026-08-01)

**Conflict Detection** ([src/lib/synthesis/conflict-detection.ts](src/lib/synthesis/conflict-detection.ts)) — spec §2.3 step 5. One LLM call reads all findings across all 5 lenses and flags genuine cross-lens contradictions (not just topically-related findings) as `{findingAId, findingBId, conflictDescription}` pairs. Never resolves anything itself — resolution is the reviewer's job. Deterministically validated: any conflict referencing a finding ID that doesn't exist in the input set (LLM hallucination — same risk class as every other lens) is dropped before it reaches the DB.

**AI Opportunity Synthesis** ([src/lib/synthesis/ai-opportunity.ts](src/lib/synthesis/ai-opportunity.ts)) — spec §2.3 step 6. Reads **reviewer-approved findings only** (confirmed interpretation: the spec numbers this step before the reviewer gate, but synthesizing opportunities from findings that might still get edited/rejected would build on sand — approved/edited findings only, never draft or rejected). Not owned by AI & Governance — confirmed when that lens was built, it explicitly excludes this output. Readiness scores (`data_quality`, `team_skill`, `process_maturity`, `governance_foundation`, each 0-3) are half deterministic, half LLM-classified: `data_quality` is computed from evidenceSufficiency aggregated across the 5 lenses, `governance_foundation` is computed directly from AI & Governance's own already-computed maturity tier (never re-derived) — only `team_skill`/`process_maturity` have no existing deterministic source, so the LLM classifies them against a fixed rubric (same classification-not-scoring pattern as AI & Governance's document-review mode). Each opportunity's `sourceFindingIds` is validated the same way as Conflict Detection — hallucinated IDs dropped, and an opportunity left with zero valid sources is dropped entirely.

**Reviewer Workspace backend** ([src/lib/reviewer/workspace.ts](src/lib/reviewer/workspace.ts)) — three distinct per-finding outcomes (confirmed: log as three, not two): `acceptFinding` → `reviewer_status = approved`, `editFinding` → `edited` (content goes in the new `reviewer_edited_content` column, `ai_draft` is never mutated), `rejectFinding` → `rejected` (dropped from client view, stays in the DB as the log). `resolveConflict` and `resolveDispute` reuse this same three-way vocabulary rather than inventing new action types — a dispute resolution is just accept/edit/reject applied to a disputed finding, plus mandatory reasoning (`dispute_resolution_notes`). `reRankTop3` reorders `reports.top_3_finding_ids` independent of editing findings. `approveReport` is a real gate, not a UI convention: it queries every finding for the report and every conflict touching those findings, and refuses to flip `pending_review` → `approved` if anything is still undecided/unresolved, returning a `blockedReason` instead of silently succeeding. `deliverReport` (approved → sent) deliberately does NOT fire a real client notification email itself — that stays a separate, explicitly-confirmed step, not an automatic side effect.

**Reviewer Queue + Workspace UI** ([src/app/(reviewer)/](<src/app/(reviewer)/>)) — the fifth internal-only area, alongside the four client-facing pages. `/queue` lists reports where the 24h edit window has closed, oldest first — still-editable reports don't show up, that's the client's business not the reviewer's yet. `/review/[reportId]` is the full workspace: findings grouped by lens, with Accept/Edit/Reject, flagged conflicts with a resolution form, disputed findings with a 3-way resolution form (keep AI's version / side with client / edit), top-3 reordering, and a real Approve button that surfaces `blockedReason` inline rather than just failing silently. Built with Next.js Server Actions (`(reviewer)/review/[reportId]/actions.ts`) calling straight into `workspace.ts` — no separate API layer. Both gaps flagged here originally (no access control; `reviewed_by` sourced from a `REVIEWER_USER_ID` env stand-in) are now closed — see "Reviewer authentication" below.

**`LensFinding` schema — four structured fields, confirmed 2026-08-01** ([src/lib/lenses/types.ts](src/lib/lenses/types.ts)): every finding across all 5 lenses now carries `diagnosis` (the observation itself), `rootCause` (WHY, genuinely causal, not a restatement), `recommendedAction` (the concrete fix), and `severity` (`critical`/`high`/`medium`/`low` — business impact if left unaddressed) as explicit, separate fields — not folded into free text. `severity` is deliberately independent of both `confidenceLevel` (how sure the lens is) and the report-level `priority_ranking` scores used for top-3 selection — a finding can be low-confidence and still critical severity. All 5 lens system prompts got a "FINDING STRUCTURE" rule block enforcing this distinction, and their zod schemas/output mappings were updated to match — verified with `tsc --noEmit`/`npm run build`/`npm run lint` all clean, and confirmed live 2026-08-02 (see the closed-out end-to-end test below): all 4 fields populated correctly across all 5 lenses in a real run, displayed and editable in the reviewer workspace.

**Reviewer Workspace UI gaps closed** (confirmed 2026-08-01, following directly from the schema change above):
- **Grouped by lens** instead of one flat list — five sections (Financial, Commercial/Market, Execution/Operating, Product/Customer, AI & Governance) in a fixed order, each only rendered if it has findings.
- **All four structured fields displayed and editable** — `FindingCard` now shows diagnosis/root cause/recommended action distinctly (not just title+rootCause), and both `EditForm` and `DisputeResolutionForm` let the reviewer edit diagnosis/rootCause/recommendedAction/severity, not just title/rootCause.
- **Mandatory-decision messaging is proactive, not just at Approve time** — a red banner at the top of the workspace counts undecided (`draft`) findings and unresolved conflicts the moment the page loads, not only when Approve is clicked and blocked; draft findings also get an amber border and a "needs decision" badge instead of a neutral gray one, so they read as something to act on rather than a passive status.
- **"Fix this first" flagging is deterministic, same pattern as every other cross-lens judgment call** ([src/lib/reviewer/prioritization.ts](src/lib/reviewer/prioritization.ts)): `isFixFirstCandidate()` flags a finding when `severity === "critical"`, or `severity === "high"` AND `goalRelevance === "directly_blocks"` — computed in code, never LLM-judged. A "Suggested fix-first (not yet in top 3)" section surfaces these with a one-click "Add to top 3" (disabled until the finding itself has been decided) — it only ever suggests, `reRankTop3` is still the reviewer's own manual call.
- **Time-per-audit instrumentation**: `reports.approved_at` (new column, `supabase/migrations/20260801080000_reports_approved_at.sql`) is stamped by `approveReport()` the instant it succeeds. The workspace header now shows two durations — full cycle (`submitted_at` → `approved_at`, measured against the 72h SLA) and reviewer-only time (`edit_window_closes_at` → `approved_at`, the part that actually scales with reviewer headcount, since the 24h client edit window isn't reviewer time).
- **Migration pushed and full workspace verified live end-to-end (2026-08-01)** — the founder ran `supabase db push` from an authenticated session; confirmed via direct query that `reports.approved_at` now exists. Full live walkthrough against a real queued report (`Nimbus Ledger Ltd`, pre-dating the 4-field schema so it doubled as a real "old-shaped data doesn't crash the new UI" test): grouping by lens rendered correctly (only lenses with findings shown), editing a finding populated diagnosis/recommendedAction/severity and immediately re-rendered with a colored severity badge and "edited" status, the mandatory-decision banner's count tracked every accept/edit/reject in real time and disappeared exactly at zero, the fix-first section appeared the instant a finding was edited to `severity: critical` and disappeared the instant it was promoted to top-3, Accept/Edit/Reject all persisted correctly (verified both in the UI and via a direct DB read), Approve succeeded once every finding was decided and correctly stamped `approved_at`/`reviewed_by` in the live DB, the workspace header's duration labels correctly switched from "→ now" to "→ approval" once approved, and the approved report correctly dropped out of `/queue` afterward (`/queue` filters `status = pending_review`).
- **Real bug found and fixed during this walkthrough**: `REVIEWER_USER_ID` was documented in `.env.local.example` but was never actually set in `.env.local` — every `approveReport` call was silently 500ing (`getReviewerId` throwing "REVIEWER_USER_ID is not set"), which is why earlier Approve clicks appeared to do nothing. Found via the browser network log (a 500 on the server action), not assumed. Fixed by pointing it at the real seeded reviewer identity already in the `users` table from the earlier blocked e2e attempt (`e2e-reviewer-...@example.com`) rather than fabricating a new one, then restarting the dev server to pick up the env change — confirmed working on the next Approve call.

**Reviewer notification timing** ([src/lib/reviewer/notifications.ts](src/lib/reviewer/notifications.ts)) — "the reviewer notification must fire the instant the 24h edit window closes" (spec §2.3a), verified as a real, deterministic, idempotent check, not just a plausible-sounding claim: `checkAndNotifyClosedEditWindows()` queries for `pending_review` reports whose `edit_window_closes_at` has passed and `reviewer_notified_at` is still null, logs a real row to the `notifications` table, and stamps `reviewer_notified_at`. Live-tested against a real report: backdating the deadline and calling the check fired exactly once (confirmed via a real `notifications` row in the DB) and calling it again immediately after correctly returned empty (no double-notify). Does not send a real outbound email as an automatic side effect of a timing check — that's a separate, explicit, confirmed step. No production cron infrastructure exists yet (Vercel Cron / pg_cron is a separate infra decision) — this is the deterministic check a cron would call on a tick, not the cron itself.

**Orchestration layer, new** ([src/lib/audit/run-audit.ts](src/lib/audit/run-audit.ts)) — ties the 5 lenses + Conflict Detection + persistence together for the first time; didn't exist before this phase. Runs all 5 lenses via `Promise.allSettled` (one lens failing must never block the others, spec §2.1 — this was proven for real, not just designed for, when a live rate-limit event failed 3 of 5 lenses mid-run and the other 2 persisted correctly regardless). Findings are persisted to `lens_findings` FIRST, then re-tagged in-memory with their real database UUID before Conflict Detection runs — everything downstream (conflicts, AI opportunities, reviewer actions) references the real UUID directly, no separate id-mapping layer. The 5 lens calls are staggered ~1.5s apart rather than fired in the exact same instant — found live that a true simultaneous burst can exceed a provider's per-minute token rate limit even when the daily quota is fine; a small stagger is cheap insurance on any tier, not just free.

**Schema additions this phase** (`supabase/migrations/20260801070706_reviewer_workspace_support.sql`, `20260801071010_lens_findings_report_id.sql`), both real gaps found while building, not anticipated upfront:
- `lens_findings.reviewer_edited_content` (jsonb) — nowhere to store what the reviewer changes a finding to; `ai_draft` had to stay immutable.
- `reports.submitted_at` / `edit_window_closes_at` / `reviewer_notified_at` — nothing tracked the 24h window or made the notification check idempotent.
- `lens_findings.report_id` (FK to `reports.id`) — **the important one**: `company_id` alone can't tell you which findings belong to which audit cycle once a company has more than one report (re-audits are an explicit product feature), so `approveReport`'s "are all of THIS report's findings resolved" check would have silently been wrong for any repeat client without this.

**Full end-to-end audit test — BLOCKED, not skipped, not faked.** Attempted with real test identities (real `auth.users` via the admin API, real `companies`/`goals` rows) and evidence deliberately engineered to plausibly produce a genuine Financial-vs-Execution margin/cost-drag conflict (the spec's own illustrative example). Confirmed along the way: `Promise.allSettled` resilience is real (a live rate-limit event took out 3 of 5 lenses mid-run, the other 2 persisted correctly), the notification timing mechanism is real and idempotent (proven against real DB rows), and the Reviewer Queue/Workspace UI correctly render real Supabase data end-to-end in-browser. What's still unverified is the single specific claim task 5 asked for — all 5 lenses succeeding together in the same run, through Conflict Detection and AI Opportunity Synthesis, with a full reviewer pass exercising every action type. Root cause, confirmed with exact numbers (not a guess): Groq's free-tier daily token cap (100k) is genuinely exhausted from this session's cumulative testing (measured at 95,991/100,000 with a rolling, not fixed-clock, reset — retry-after varied 19–26 minutes across identical-time calls). One unexplained event during this attempt: a partial-run report's findings briefly showed reviewer dispositions (edited/approved/rejected) that no traceable code path in this session set — browser network logs showed only GET requests, no scratch script got far enough to call any reviewer action, and no stray process was found running. Reset to a clean draft state before proceeding rather than build on state that couldn't be attributed to anything — flagged here rather than silently ignored; unresolved, watch for recurrence.

**Two more full-run attempts, 2026-08-01, after quota reset — same real gap found twice, then fixed.** Ran `scratch-e2e-run2.ts` twice once Groq's daily quota freed up. Both runs: Execution, Product, Commercial, and AI & Governance all succeeded and persisted correctly, real conflicts were detected (1 on the first run, 2 more new ones on the second — including a low-value one worth noting: the LLM flagged an "AI summary feature in production" finding against a "live AI in production" finding as a conflict, but its own description admits "there are no direct conflicts... they both acknowledge the presence of AI" — a flagged-but-not-genuine conflict, which is exactly what human review exists to catch, not a system failure), the disputed Commercial finding got marked correctly, and the notification check fired. But Financial failed both times with the identical error, not random variance: `goalRelevance: "directly_affects"` — the literal same out-of-enum hallucination documented as the original motivation for `generateValidatedJson`. Isolated the Financial call directly (with temporary raw-response logging, reverted after) and confirmed it's reproducible with this evidence: two genuinely healthy findings (76% gross margin vs. 70% floor, 14mo runway vs. 12mo threshold) under goal `cash_flow_margin_efficiency` — none of the original three `goalRelevance` values (`directly_blocks`/`indirectly_affects`/`unrelated`) honestly describe "this is good news and directly relevant to the goal," so the model invented one instead of picking the closest existing option.

**Fixed at the schema level, not just the prompt** (confirmed 2026-08-01, explicit direction: the enum was genuinely incomplete for a common real case, prompt wording is reinforcement on top of that fix, not a substitute for it): added `"directly_supports"` as a fourth `GoalRelevance` value ([src/lib/lenses/types.ts](src/lib/lenses/types.ts), [src/lib/lenses/schemas.ts](src/lib/lenses/schemas.ts)) — a genuinely healthy/positive finding that's directly and materially relevant to the goal. Updated everywhere the enum is load-bearing: all 5 lens system prompts (rule 5/6 guidance + OUTPUT SCHEMA JSON), the reviewer workspace's edit-form dropdown, and `run-audit.ts`'s deterministic `scoreForDefaultRanking` weight map (`directly_blocks: 3, directly_supports: 2, indirectly_affects: 1, unrelated: 0` — blocks still outranks supports for top-3 purposes, since top-3 is about what needs action, not just goal-relevance). AI & Governance's prompt was updated to explain (not contradict) why `directly_supports` will rarely apply there — that lens's rule 4 already forbids "things are healthy" findings by design, so the new value mostly won't come up, but the schema still needed it for shared-type correctness. `tsc --noEmit`/`npm run build`/`npm run lint` all clean. **Not yet re-verified live** — the isolated retry hit a fresh 429 (95,435/100,000 daily tokens used, ~19m29s retry-after, confirmed via the actual Groq error payload, not assumed) before the fix itself could be confirmed against the exact evidence that broke it twice. This is quota exhaustion, a different failure mode than the bug just fixed — do not treat a future 429 as evidence the enum fix didn't work.

**`directly_supports` fix confirmed live, twice, 2026-08-02** — retried after a ~20min wait (Groq's reset really is rolling, not fixed-clock: a cheap probe call succeeded well before the quoted ~59min retry-after would have elapsed, so the wait was cut short based on that signal rather than the literal countdown). First retry: quota ran out mid-run before Execution/Product/Commercial/AI & Governance could all complete (only Financial persisted — 2 findings, correctly tagged `directly_supports`, passed validation), confirming the fix works but not yet a full run. Second retry (immediately after, quota still had some headroom): 3/5 succeeded together — Financial (again, no enum error), Product, AI & Governance — plus Conflict Detection ran cleanly and found a real cross-lens conflict. Execution and Commercial failed this specific run; **isolated both directly afterward and both succeeded standalone with the exact same evidence** — confirming that failure was transient per-minute rate-limiting from the 5 staggered-but-still-overlapping calls, not a reproducible bug like Financial's was. Net result: **every one of the 5 lenses has now individually succeeded live on 2026-08-01/02 evidence**, and Conflict Detection/dispute-marking/notification-timing have each fired correctly in multiple runs — the only thing not yet proven is all 5 landing in the exact same single run, which looks like free-tier per-minute throttling variance rather than any code or design gap.

**Full end-to-end audit test — CLOSED OUT, 2026-08-02.** One more retry immediately after the above, while quota still had headroom: a completely clean run — `failedLenses: []`, all 5 lenses succeeded together (18 findings: 2 financial, 3 execution, 3 product, 2 commercial, 8 ai_governance), Conflict Detection found a real new cross-lens conflict, the Commercial dispute got marked, and the notification check fired (report `d7c28f88-2c54-401e-83ce-620cddd2abe1`). Immediately followed with a full reviewer pass on this exact report (`src/lib/reviewer/workspace.ts` functions called directly, not re-driven through the browser — the UI layer for these same actions was already proven separately in the 2026-08-01 walkthrough on a different report): `editFinding` (1), `rejectFinding` (1), `acceptFinding` (15) — all three dispositions exercised — then `resolveDispute` (kept the AI's independent version), `resolveConflict`, `reRankTop3`, and `approveReport`, which succeeded (`{approved: true}`) once everything was resolved. Then ran `synthesizeAiOpportunities` against the 17 real reviewer-approved findings: 3 genuine opportunities came back, all correctly citing real finding IDs, all reasonably tagged `fix_groundwork_first` given the nascent governance maturity. Every piece of the original 5-part Phase 2 request is now proven against one real, complete run — not five separate partial proofs stitched together in the writeup. Note: the older partial-run report used for the 2026-08-01 Reviewer Workspace UI walkthrough (`d8f84c84-...`) remains genuinely `approved` in the live DB as a side effect of that earlier verification pass — a real approval, not test debris.
- **One real gap surfaced doing this, not fixed, flagged for later**: `evidenceSufficiencyByLens` and `governanceMaturityTier` — both required by `synthesizeAiOpportunities` for its readiness scoring — are only ever produced in-memory during `runAudit()` and are never persisted anywhere. Since AI Opportunity Synthesis is designed to run *after* reviewer approval (which can happen hours or days later, in a completely separate process), there's currently no way for a real trigger to reconstruct those inputs from DB state alone. This verification run used reasonable approximations (`governanceMaturityTier` was known from the run's own printed output; `evidenceSufficiencyByLens` was estimated) rather than the exact original values — good enough to prove the function works correctly against real approved findings, not a substitute for real persistence.
